\section{Homological images and aggregate quotients}
\label{sec:homological-images}

Fix a finite simplicial complex \(N\), a finite abelian group \(A\), and a
class \(\omega\in H^2(N,A)\).  The universal coefficient map is denoted by
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_\omega:
 H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
\]
Define its \emph{homological image} and \emph{homological cokernel} by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:homological-image-cokernel}
 K_\omega:=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega),
 \qquad Q_\omega:=A/K_\omega.
\end{equation}
These definitions depend on the supplied finite presentation of the component
gerbe.  Refinement and retrivialization transport them by UCT naturality, but
no canonical identification along distinct comparison zigzags is asserted.
The resulting freely generated presentation groupoid and its elementary
naturality calculation are recorded in the technical appendix rather than
promoted to an invariance theorem.

For a finite family of component classes
\((\omega_v)_{v\in V}\), put \(K_v=K_{\omega_v}\) and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-quotients}
 Q_{\mathrm{lab}}:=A\Big/\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v,
 \qquad
 Q_{\mathrm{com}}:=A\Big/\sum_{v\in V}K_v.
\end{equation}
The first quotient retains enough information to map to every componentwise
quotient \(A/K_v\).  The second retains only characters which descend through
every one of them.

\begin{proposition}[UCT and aggregate subgroup package]
\label{prop:uct-aggregate-package}
With the notation above:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item The universal coefficient sequence is
      \[
      0\longrightarrow
      \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)
      \longrightarrow H^2(N,A)
      \xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}
      \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
      \longrightarrow0.
      \]
      Thus \(K_\omega=0\) exactly when \(\omega\) lies in the displayed
      Ext subgroup.  In particular, nonzero classes can have zero
      homological image when \(H_1\) has torsion.
\item The quotient map \(q_\omega:A\to Q_\omega\) is initial among group
      homomorphisms \(q:A\to B\) satisfying
      \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\).  A character
      \(\chi:A\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z\) factors through \(Q_\omega\)
      exactly when \(\chi(K_\omega)=0\).  Since finite Pontryagin duality
      separates points,
      \[
      K_\omega=\mathop{\bigcap}_{\chi(K_\omega)=0}\ker\chi.
      \]
\item For a nonempty finite family \((K_v)_{v\in V}\), there is a natural
      short exact sequence
      \begin{equation}\label{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}
      0\longrightarrow
      \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}
      \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{lab}}
      \longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{com}}
      \longrightarrow0.
      \end{equation}
      Moreover,
      \[
      \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{com}}}
      \cong\bigcap_{v\in V}K_v^\perp,
      \qquad
      \widehat{Q_{\mathrm{lab}}}
      \cong\sum_{v\in V}K_v^\perp
      \]
      inside \(\widehat A\).
\item If \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) and
      \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\), a subgroup
      \(K\le A\) occurs as \(K_\omega\) exactly when \(d(K)\le\beta\).
      Every finite family of such subgroups is simultaneously attained by
      the prestack of
      \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Clause (i) is the cohomological universal coefficient theorem, natural in
both \(N\) and \(A\) \cite[Sec.~3.1]{Hatcher2002}.  Its middle-to-right map
evaluates a cohomology class on integral \(2\)-cycles, so its value at
\(\omega\) is \(\operatorname{ev}_\omega\).  Exactness identifies the
kernel with the Ext term.

For a simplicial map \(f:N'\to N\), naturality is the commutative square
\[
\begin{CD}
H^2(N,A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
 \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)\\
@V{f^*}VV @VV{(-)\circ f_*}V\\
H^2(N',A) @>{\operatorname{ev}}>>
 \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N',\mathbb Z),A).
\end{CD}
\]
Thus
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_{f^*\omega}
 =\operatorname{ev}_\omega\circ f_*.
\]
For a coefficient homomorphism \(\theta:A\to A'\), the corresponding
identity is
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_{\theta_*\omega}
 =\theta\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega.
\]
These formulas are the only invariance input used for a specified
presentation comparison.  The short exact UCT sequence may split as a
sequence of groups, but no natural splitting is chosen.

For (ii), \(q\circ\operatorname{ev}_\omega=0\) says precisely that \(q\)
vanishes on \(K_\omega\).  The universal property of the quotient gives a
unique factorization through \(A/K_\omega\).  Apply the same observation to
characters.  The intersection formula follows because characters of a
finite abelian group separate points \cite{Terras1999}.

For the last assertion, pass to the finite quotient \(A/K_\omega\).  If
\(a\notin K_\omega\), then \(\bar a\ne0\) in that quotient.  Finite
Pontryagin duality gives a character
\[
 \bar\chi:A/K_\omega\longrightarrow\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z
\]
with \(\bar\chi(\bar a)\ne0\).  Its pullback \(\chi=\bar\chi q_\omega\)
annihilates \(K_\omega\) but not \(a\).  Hence no element outside
\(K_\omega\) belongs to the displayed intersection of kernels.  The reverse
containment is immediate.

The containment \(\bigcap_vK_v\subseteq\sum_vK_v\) gives the quotient map in
\eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence}.  By the third isomorphism theorem its
kernel is
\[
 \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v},
\]
which proves exactness.  For finite abelian groups, annihilators exchange sums and
intersections:
\[
 \Big(\sum_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\bigcap_vK_v^\perp,
 \qquad
 \Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp=\sum_vK_v^\perp.
\]
Dualizing the two quotients proves the character formulas in (iii).

The first annihilator identity does not require finiteness:
\(\chi\) kills \(\sum_vK_v\) exactly when it kills every \(K_v\).  For the
second, one inclusion is immediate:
\[
 \sum_vK_v^\perp\subseteq\Big(\bigcap_vK_v\Big)^\perp.
\]
For finite groups, equality follows from exactness of Pontryagin duality.
It may also be checked by cardinalities, using
\[
 |H^\perp|=\frac{|A|}{|H|}
\]
and, for two subgroups,
\[
 |K+L|\,|K\cap L|=|K|\,|L|.
\]
Induction gives the formula for a finite family.

Under the hypotheses of (iv), the Ext term vanishes and UCT becomes
\[
 H^2(N,A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb Z^\beta,A).
\]
The image of a homomorphism from \(\mathbb Z^\beta\) is generated by the
images of the standard basis, so it has at most \(\beta\) generators.
Conversely, given generators \(k_1,\dots,k_\beta\) of \(K\), allowing zeros
when fewer are needed, the map
\[
 (n_1,\dots,n_\beta)\longmapsto\sum_jn_jk_j
\]
has image \(K\).  Applying
\Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} to a family of these maps
gives simultaneous realization.

The number \(d(K)\) can be read prime by prime.  If
\[
 K\cong\bigoplus_p\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_p}C_{p^{e_{p,j}}},
\]
then
\[
 d(K)=\max_p r_p.
\]
Thus the condition in (iv) is not a bound on \(|K|\) or on its exponent.
It is precisely the rank bound imposed by the \(\beta\) basis elements of
\(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\).  Once homomorphisms with the desired images have been
chosen componentwise, simultaneous attainment is the prestack-level part
supplied by \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{How to read the aggregate quotients}

There are canonical maps
\[
 Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\longrightarrow A/K_v
 \qquad(v\in V),
\]
because \(\bigcap_wK_w\subseteq K_v\).  The product of these maps is
injective:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:labelled-product-embedding}
 A/\bigcap_vK_v
 \longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V}A/K_v,
 \qquad
 a+\bigcap_vK_v\longmapsto(a+K_v)_v.
\end{equation}
Its kernel consists of the elements lying in every \(K_v\).  Thus
\(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\) is the part of the coefficient group retained by all
componentwise quotient values together.

In the other direction, every componentwise quotient maps onto
\(Q_{\mathrm{com}}\):
\[
 A/K_v\longrightarrow A/\sum_wK_w,
\]
because \(K_v\subseteq\sum_wK_w\).  This is the largest quotient of \(A\)
of the displayed form receiving the quotient map from every \(A/K_v\).
The map in \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} measures the loss between
keeping the tuple of component values and keeping only their common quotient.

On characters, the directions reverse.  A character of
\(Q_{\mathrm{com}}\) must kill every \(K_v\), while a character of
\(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\) is a finite sum of characters each killing one of the
component subgroups.  Written inside \(\widehat A\), these are precisely the
two annihilator identities in the proposition.  This interpretation uses
only the contravariance of finite duality.

\subsection*{Evaluation at chain level}

Choose a simplicial cocycle
\(\alpha\in Z^2(N,A)\) representing \(\omega\).  If
\[
 z=\sum_{\sigma\in N_2}n_\sigma\sigma
\]
is an integral \(2\)-cycle, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain-level-evaluation}
 \operatorname{ev}_\omega([z])
 =\langle\alpha,z\rangle
 =\sum_{\sigma\in N_2}n_\sigma\alpha(\sigma).
\end{equation}
Replacing \(\alpha\) by \(\alpha+\delta b\) changes the last expression by
\[
 \langle\delta b,z\rangle
 =\langle b,\partial z\rangle=0.
\]
Replacing \(z\) by \(z+\partial c\) changes it by
\[
 \langle\alpha,\partial c\rangle
 =\langle\delta\alpha,c\rangle=0.
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:chain-level-evaluation} depends only on the cohomology class
and the homology class.  In the realization theorem,
\(\alpha=\alpha_\lambda\) is also the triple-overlap defect of the
\(\lambda\)-component gerbe, so the formula evaluates that defect directly
on a \(2\)-cycle of the chosen nerve.

This chain formula also explains why the coordinate subgroup
\[
 H_\alpha=\langle\alpha(\sigma):\sigma\in N_2\rangle
\]
can be larger than \(K_\omega\).  Every value in \(K_\omega\) is an integral
combination of cocycle coordinates, so \(K_\omega\subseteq H_\alpha\).
But the coefficients in \eqref{eq:chain-level-evaluation} must satisfy the
cycle equations.  An arbitrary combination of coordinates need not arise
from a cycle.  Moreover, \(H_\alpha\) can change when \(\alpha\) is altered
by a coboundary, whereas \(K_\omega\) cannot.

\subsection*{Torsion and free-homology boundary cases}

Suppose
\[
 H_1(N,\mathbb Z)
 \cong\mathbb Z^r\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^t\mathbb Z/n_j\mathbb Z.
\]
The free summand contributes nothing to the Ext term, while
\[
 \operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathbb Z/n_j\mathbb Z,A)
 \cong A/n_jA.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:explicit-ext-term}
 \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 \cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^t A/n_jA.
\end{equation}
If every multiplication map \(n_j:A\to A\) is surjective, the Ext term
vanishes even though \(H_1\) has torsion.  For finite \(A\), shared primary
torsion between \(H_1\) and \(A\) is what permits classes that evaluation on
\(H_2\) cannot detect.

For example, if
\[
 H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 \qquad H_2(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
 \qquad A=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
\]
then \(H^2(N,A)\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\) entirely through
\eqref{eq:explicit-ext-term}.  The nonzero class defines a non-neutral
banded gerbe, but
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_\omega:0\longrightarrow A
\]
has zero image and \(Q_\omega=A\).  The quotient does not detect neutrality
in this case.

At the opposite endpoint, if \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\) is free, the Ext term
vanishes.  Then
\[
 H^2(N,A)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
\]
is an isomorphism.  A class has zero homological image only when it is zero.
The wedge-of-spheres application lies in this regime.

\subsection*{A two-component arithmetic check}

Let
\[
 A=\mathbb Z/12\mathbb Z,\qquad
 K_1=\langle4\rangle,\qquad
 K_2=\langle3\rangle.
\]
Then \(K_1\) has order \(3\), \(K_2\) has order \(4\), and
\[
 K_1\cap K_2=0,\qquad K_1+K_2=A.
\]
Hence
\[
 Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0,
\]
and the kernel in \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} is \(A\).  Both
subgroups are cyclic, so each is the image of a homomorphism
\(\mathbb Z\to A\).  On a nerve with
\(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z\) and \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\), the
prescribed-realization theorem places these maps in two component gerbes of
a single prestack.

By contrast, if \(A=\mathbb Z/8\mathbb Z\), any two nonzero subgroups are
comparable:
\[
 \langle4\rangle\subseteq\langle2\rangle\subseteq A.
\]
Their intersection cannot be zero.  Thus no two nonzero component images
can satisfy \(K_1\cap K_2=0\) and \(K_1+K_2=A\).  This elementary contrast
is the cyclic part of the arithmetic used in the next section.

All clauses except simultaneous prestack realization are standard formal
consequences of UCT, finite duality, and subgroup arithmetic.  In particular,
there is no separate information-theoretic theorem hidden in the fibres of
these quotients.  Since a quotient fibre is a coset of its kernel, the minimum
cardinality of an unrestricted auxiliary set capable of separating every
fibre is simply the kernel cardinality.  For example,
\[
 |q_\omega^{-1}(\bar a)|=|K_\omega|,
 \qquad
 \left|\ker(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\to Q_{\mathrm{com}})\right|
 =\left|\frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}\right|.
\]

The Ext clause also marks a limitation of the homological image.  If
\(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) and
\(\operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)\ne0\), a nonzero gerbe class can
have \(K_\omega=0\) and \(Q_\omega=A\).  For instance, an
\(\mathbb RP^2\)-type nerve and suitable \(2\)-torsion coefficients exhibit
this behavior.  Conversely, when \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)\) is free, the Ext term
vanishes and zero evaluation forces \(\omega=0\).  These are UCT boundary
cases, not additional classification results.


 succeeded in 205ms:
\section{The wedge-of-spheres realization corollary}
\label{sec:wedge-application}

Fix \(\beta\ge1\), and let \(K_\beta\) be a finite connected simplicial
complex with
\[
 |K_\beta|\simeq\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2.
\]
For each \(m\ge0\), take the open-star cover of
\(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\), and let \(\mathcal B_\beta\) be the
all-subdivision basis site of
\Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison}(iii).  Every nonempty finite overlap is
contractible, hence connected, and the coherent coefficient trivializations
are those of the genuine constant sheaf.  Thus the hypothesis omitted by an
ordinary-nerve formulation with disconnected overlaps is satisfied here.

\subsection*{The selected geometric presentation}

The vertices of \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\) index its open-star cover.
A finite family of open stars has nonempty intersection exactly when the
corresponding vertices span a simplex.  The nerve of the cover is therefore
canonically isomorphic to \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta\).  In particular,
\[
 |N(\mathcal U^{(m)})|
 \cong|\operatorname{Sd}^mK_\beta|
 \cong|K_\beta|.
\]
The homology groups used below are consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wedge-homology}
 H_1(N(\mathcal U^{(m)}),\mathbb Z)=0,
 \qquad
 H_2(N(\mathcal U^{(m)}),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta.
\end{equation}

If vertices \(v_0,\dots,v_q\) span a simplex, the intersection of their open
stars is the open star of that simplex.  It deformation retracts to the
barycentre and is connected and contractible.  If they do not span a
simplex, their intersection is empty and is represented by the strict
initial object of the finite intersection site.  Hence each selected cover
satisfies the connected-overlap hypothesis literally, not only after passage
to a component nerve.

The subdivision maps provide a filtered system of refinements.  The basis
comparison does not assert that a homological image subgroup is independent
of every possible refinement zigzag.  It says that each class on the
underlying polyhedron has representatives on sufficiently fine selected
covers and that the associated derived class is the image of those
representatives.  The corollary below fixes one such cover and applies the
realization theorem there.

For two component classes with homological images \(K_1,K_2\le G\), the
kernel of the aggregate comparison is, by
\eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence},
\[
 \frac{K_1+K_2}{K_1\cap K_2}.
\]
We ask when the selected realization construction makes this kernel equal to
the band itself by requiring
\begin{equation}\label{eq:complementary-images}
 K_1\cap K_2=0,
 \qquad K_1+K_2=G,
 \qquad K_1,K_2\ne0.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Two-component realization on a wedge of \(2\)-spheres]
\label{thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss}
Let \(G\) be a finite abelian group and let \(d(G)\) be its minimal number
of generators.  The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item \(G\ne0\), \(d(G)\le2\beta\), and \(G\) is not cyclic of
      prime-power order;
\item on one of the selected finite open-star covers in
      \(\mathcal B_\beta\), the prestack construction of
      \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} has two non-neutral
      component gerbes whose homological images satisfy
      \eqref{eq:complementary-images}.
\end{enumerate}
When these conditions hold,
\[
 Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\cong G,
 \qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0,
 \qquad
 \ker(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\to Q_{\mathrm{com}})\cong G,
\]
and the constructed component presheaf has matching without amalgamation.
The assertion is relative to this selected finite-site realization; it is not
a presentation-independent classification of gerbes or finite abelian groups.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For every selected cover, the nerve is a barycentric subdivision of
\(K_\beta\).  The nerve and basis-site comparisons of
\Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison} give
\[
 H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
 \qquad
 H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta,
\]
and identify the finite Cech representatives with the corresponding derived
classes on \(\mathcal B_\beta\).

Assume (ii).  By \Cref{prop:uct-aggregate-package}(iv), each \(K_i\) is
generated by at most \(\beta\) elements.  The complementary-image equations
give
\[
 G=K_1\oplus K_2.
\]
Hence \(d(G)\le d(K_1)+d(K_2)\le2\beta\).  Since both summands are nonzero,
\(G\) is decomposable.  The indecomposable finite abelian groups are exactly
the cyclic groups of prime-power order \cite[Chap.~12]{DummitFoote2004}, so
(i) follows.

For completeness, the generator inequality may be checked prime by prime.
For a finite abelian group \(H\), let
\[
 r_p(H)=\dim_{\mathbb F_p}(H/pH).
\]
Then \(d(H)=\max_p r_p(H)\).  Direct sums give
\[
 r_p(K_1\oplus K_2)=r_p(K_1)+r_p(K_2),
\]
and hence
\[
 d(G)
 =\max_p\bigl(r_p(K_1)+r_p(K_2)\bigr)
 \le d(K_1)+d(K_2)
 \le2\beta.
\]
This proves the numerical necessity without choosing an invariant-factor
basis.

Conversely, suppose (i).  If \(d(G)=r\ge2\), choose an invariant-factor
decomposition
\[
 G\cong C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_r},
 \qquad n_1\mid n_2\mid\cdots\mid n_r,
\]
and let \(t=\min\{\beta,r-1\}\).  Put
\[
 K_1=C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_t},
 \qquad
 K_2=C_{n_{t+1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_r}.
\]
Both groups are nonzero.  We have \(d(K_1)\le\beta\), while
\(d(K_2)=r-t\le\beta\) because \(r\le2\beta\).

If \(d(G)=1\), then \(G\) is cyclic.  Its order is not a prime power, so it
has coprime factors \(a,b>1\).  The Chinese remainder theorem gives
\[
 G\cong C_a\oplus C_b.
\]
Taking \(K_1=C_a\) and \(K_2=C_b\) again gives two nonzero summands, each
generated by one element.  Thus in either case
\(G=K_1\oplus K_2\), with \(d(K_i)\le\beta\).

In the case \(r\ge2\), the chosen invariant-factor decomposition makes the
generator counts transparent.  The first block has \(t\le\beta\) cyclic
factors.  If \(r\le\beta\), then \(t=r-1\) and the second block is cyclic.
If \(r>\beta\), then \(t=\beta\) and
\[
 r-t=r-\beta\le\beta
\]
because \(r=d(G)\le2\beta\).  Thus neither block is empty and both satisfy
the rank constraint required by the UCT package.

In the cyclic case, write
\[
 |G|=\prod_{p\mid |G|}p^{e_p}
\]
with at least two distinct primes.  Partition the prime set into two
nonempty subsets \(P_1,P_2\), put
\[
 a=\prod_{p\in P_1}p^{e_p},
 \qquad
 b=\prod_{p\in P_2}p^{e_p},
\]
and use \(C_{ab}\cong C_a\oplus C_b\).  This gives the required summands
without assuming a particular factorization in advance.

Choose homomorphisms
\[
 \phi_i:\mathbb Z^\beta\longrightarrow G
\]
with images \(K_i\), and transport them across
\(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\).  The finite-site realization
theorem, with label set \(\{1,2\}\), produces the two component gerbes and
their prescribed images.  Both maps are nonzero, so both gerbes are
non-neutral and the component presheaf has matching without amalgamation.
Finally, \eqref{eq:aggregate-quotients} and
\eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} give the three displayed quotient
identities.

To make the maps explicit, choose generators
\[
 K_i=\langle k_{i1},\dots,k_{i r_i}\rangle,
 \qquad r_i\le\beta,
\]
and send the first \(r_i\) standard basis vectors of \(\mathbb Z^\beta\)
to the displayed generators, sending the remaining basis vectors to zero.
The UCT isomorphism selects classes \(\omega_i\in H^2(N,G)\), and the
construction of
\Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} selects normalized
cocycles and places them in the two labelled summands of one prestack.
Since \(K_i\ne0\), neither evaluation map is zero, so both Giraud classes are
nonzero in the \(H_1=0\) regime.
\end{proof}

For \(\beta=1\), the statement says that a nonzero finite abelian group is
attained in this two-component manner precisely when it splits as a direct
sum of two nonzero cyclic groups.  This includes cyclic groups whose orders
have at least two distinct prime divisors and noncyclic two-generated groups,
but excludes every cyclic \(p\)-group.  This small case illustrates that the
criterion is the sharp arithmetic endpoint of the chosen realization, not a
new structural theorem about finite abelian groups.

\subsection*{Low-rank cases}

For \(\beta=1\), both images must be cyclic.  The admissible groups fall into
two families.

First, a cyclic group \(C_n\) is admissible exactly when \(n\) has at least
two distinct prime divisors.  If \(n=ab\) with \(\gcd(a,b)=1\) and
\(a,b>1\), take
\[
 K_1=\langle b\rangle\cong C_a,
 \qquad
 K_2=\langle a\rangle\cong C_b
\]
inside \(C_{ab}\).  They have zero intersection and generate the group.
If \(n=p^e\), the subgroup lattice is a chain and no two nonzero subgroups
have zero intersection.

Second, a noncyclic two-generated group is admissible whenever it decomposes
as a direct sum of two nonzero cyclic groups.  For example,
\[
 G=C_4\oplus C_2,\qquad
 K_1=C_4\oplus0,\qquad K_2=0\oplus C_2.
\]
Each image is generated by one element of \(H_2\cong\mathbb Z\).  On the
other hand, \(C_2^3\) requires three generators and violates
\(d(G)\le2\), even though it has many complementary subgroups.

For \(\beta=2\), groups with at most four generators are permitted, provided
they are not cyclic of prime-power order.  For instance,
\[
 G=C_2^4
\]
is realized by the complementary rank-two subgroups
\[
 K_1=\langle e_1,e_2\rangle,
 \qquad
 K_2=\langle e_3,e_4\rangle.
\]
Each is the image of \(\mathbb Z^2\).  The bound is sharp:
\(C_2^5\) cannot be written as a sum of two subgroups each generated by at
most two elements, since their sum would have rank at most four.

Mixed-primary examples do not require the same decomposition at each prime.
Take
\[
 G=C_4\oplus C_2\oplus C_3.
\]
For \(\beta=1\), the decomposition
\[
 K_1=C_4,\qquad K_2=C_2\oplus C_3\cong C_6
\]
has two cyclic summands, so the group is already admissible.  This illustrates
why \(d(G)\) is the maximum primary rank rather than the total number of
cyclic factors appearing in an arbitrary decomposition.

In every example, the conclusion remains about the selected prestack
realization.  The arithmetic chooses possible image subgroups; the
finite-site theorem supplies component gerbes having those images.  Neither
step classifies all gerbes on the wedge or assigns such data to an unrelated
semantic model.


 succeeded in 227ms:
\section{Component gerbes and terminal rigidity}
\label{sec:gerbe-obstruction}

For a stack in groupoids \(\mathfrak E\), let \(\pi_0(\mathfrak E)\) denote
the sheafification of its presheaf of fibrewise isomorphism classes.  If
\(v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak E)(a)\), define the full substack
\begin{equation}\label{eq:component-substack}
 \mathfrak E[v](X)=
 \{x\in\mathfrak E(X):[x]=v|_X\}.
\end{equation}
The equality in \eqref{eq:component-substack} is equality in the component
sheaf, so it is local by construction.

The exact ancestry of this construction is classical.  Giraud proves that
the projection of a stack to its sheaf of connected components is a gerbe
over that sheaf, and that pullback along a component section gives the
corresponding maximal subgerbe
\cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  The next proposition records this
standard construction and the two compatibilities used later.  It is an
input, not an originality claim.

\begin{proposition}[Standard component-gerbe package]
\label{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}
Let \(\mathfrak E\) be a stack in groupoids on \(\mathcal C\), and let
\(v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak E)(a)\).
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item The full substack \(\mathfrak E[v]\) is the maximal gerbe over the
      section \(v\).  If \(\mathfrak E\) is banded by an abelian sheaf
      \(\mathscr A\), then \(\mathfrak E[v]\) has the restricted
      \(\mathscr A\)-banding.
\item Its Giraud class satisfies
      \[
      [\mathfrak E[v]]=0\text{ in }H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
      \mathfrak E[v]\text{ is neutral}
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
      \mathfrak E[v](a)\ne\varnothing.
      \]
\item Suppose \(\Phi:\mathfrak E\to\mathfrak E'\) is a banded equivalence,
      its band map is \(\theta:\mathscr A\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr A'\),
      and its component map carries \(v\) to \(v'\).  Then \(\Phi\)
      restricts to a banded equivalence
      \(\mathfrak E[v]\simeq\mathfrak E'[v']\), and
      \[
      \theta_*[\mathfrak E[v]]=[\mathfrak E'[v']].
      \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Clause (i) is precisely the component-gerbe construction of
\cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  Concretely, \(v|_X\) is locally
represented by an object, so \(\mathfrak E[v]\) is locally nonempty; two of
its objects have the same component class and are therefore locally
isomorphic.  Fullness preserves the automorphism sheaves and hence the band.

The classification of abelian-banded gerbes identifies equivalence classes
with \(H^2\), with the neutral gerbes representing zero
\cite[Chap.~IV]{Giraud1971}.  On a site with terminal object \(a\), neutrality
is exactly existence of an object over \(a\), proving (ii).  For (iii), the
component compatibility makes \(\Phi\) restrict to the full component
substacks.  Applying \(\Phi\) to local objects and overlap arrows applies
\(\theta\) to their triple-overlap defect cocycle.  This is the standard
naturality of Giraud classification under a banded equivalence
\cite[Tag 06NY]{StacksProject}.
\end{proof}

Apply this package to the stackification
\(\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\) of \Cref{sec:preliminaries}.  In view of
\eqref{eq:stackification-components-core}, every
\(v\in(aF)(a)\) determines the component gerbe
\[
 \mathfrak G_v:=\mathfrak L[v]
\]
and, when \(\mathfrak P\) is \(\mathscr A\)-banded, a class
\[
 \omega_v:=[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A).
\]
On a Leray cover, choosing local objects and overlap arrows produces the
usual Cech \(2\)-cocycle; changing either choice changes it by a coboundary.
This is the local representative of the same Giraud class, not a second
obstruction construction \cite{Breen2006,Moerdijk2002}.

\subsection*{The standard cocycle in local coordinates}

We spell out the preceding sentence because the realization theorem will use
the same coordinates in the opposite direction.  Let
\(\mathcal U=\{U_i\to a\}\) be a cover on which
\(\mathfrak G_v\) has objects \(x_i\in\mathfrak G_v(U_i)\).  After refining
the cover when necessary, choose isomorphisms
\[
 g_{ij}:x_i|_{U_{ij}}\longrightarrow x_j|_{U_{ij}},
 \qquad
 g_{ii}=1,\quad g_{ji}=g_{ij}^{-1}.
\]
On a triple overlap \(U_{ijk}\), the two arrows from \(x_i\) to \(x_k\)
need not agree.  Their ratio is an automorphism of \(x_i\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:giraud-triple-defect}
 c_{ijk}
 :=g_{ik}^{-1}g_{jk}g_{ij}
 \in\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(x_i)(U_{ijk})
 \cong\mathscr A(U_{ijk}).
\end{equation}
The last identification is the chosen banding.  We write the band
additively.  Comparing the five composites around a quadruple overlap gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:giraud-cocycle-equation}
 c_{jkl}-c_{ikl}+c_{ijl}-c_{ijk}=0.
\end{equation}
Thus \(c=(c_{ijk})\) is a Cech \(2\)-cocycle.

There are two relevant changes of choice.  First, keep the local objects
fixed and replace the overlap arrows by
\[
 g'_{ij}=b_{ij}\cdot g_{ij},
 \qquad b_{ij}\in\mathscr A(U_{ij}).
\]
Because the band is abelian, substituting in
\eqref{eq:giraud-triple-defect} gives
\[
 c'_{ijk}
 =c_{ijk}+b_{jk}-b_{ik}+b_{ij}
 =c_{ijk}+(\delta b)_{ijk}.
\]
Second, replace \(x_i\) by an isomorphic local object \(x'_i\) and choose
arrows \(h_i:x_i\to x'_i\).  Transporting \(g_{ij}\) by the \(h_i\) gives
overlap arrows for the new objects with the same defect under the band
identification.  Any other choice of transported overlap arrows differs
from these by a \(1\)-cochain and therefore changes the defect by a
coboundary.  Refining the cover pulls the cocycle back.  The resulting
class is consequently independent of all local choices in the Cech colimit,
and the Leray comparison sends it to
\([\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).

The neutral case is visible in the same coordinates.  A global object
\(x\in\mathfrak G_v(a)\) restricts to local objects whose canonical overlap
arrows have zero triple defect.  Conversely, if the defect of a chosen local
system is a coboundary, modifying its overlap arrows by the corresponding
\(1\)-cochain makes the defect zero.  Effective descent in the stack then
glues the local objects to a global object.  This is the cocycle-level
description of the neutrality equivalence in
\Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(ii); it is not a replacement
for Giraud classification on a general site.

Finally, if a banded equivalence has band map
\(\theta:\mathscr A\to\mathscr A'\), it sends the chosen local objects and
arrows to local objects and arrows whose defect is \(\theta(c_{ijk})\).
This makes the naturality in
\Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(iii) literal on a common
presentation.

\begin{theorem}[Presheaf-to-component-gerbe comparison criterion]
\label{thm:gerbe-null-semantics}
Let \(F\) be separated on the slice over \(a\), and let
\(\mathfrak P\) be an abelian-banded prestack lift whose stackification map
is terminally essentially surjective.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:matching-gerbe-criterion}
 \operatorname{MWA}(F,a)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (aF)(a)\ne\varnothing
 \ \text{and}\
 \mathfrak G_v\text{ is non-neutral for every }v\in(aF)(a).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, every \(\omega_v\) is nonzero.  The dependencies are
\[
\begin{array}{c@{\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad}c}
 \operatorname{Match}(F,a)&(aF)(a)\ne\varnothing
 \quad\text{(slice separatedness)},\\[2mm]
 v\in(aF)(a)&\mathfrak G_v\text{ a component gerbe}
 \quad\text{(Giraud III.2.1.5.3)},\\[2mm]
 F(a)\ne\varnothing&\mathfrak L(a)\ne\varnothing
 \quad\text{(terminal essential surjectivity)},\\[2mm]
 \mathfrak G_v(a)\ne\varnothing&\mathfrak G_v\text{ neutral}
 \quad\text{(Giraud classification)}.
\end{array}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i), matching is equivalent to
\((aF)(a)\ne\varnothing\).  By clause (ii) of that proposition and terminal
essential surjectivity, \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\) is equivalent to
\(\mathfrak L(a)\ne\varnothing\).  The latter holds exactly when one of the
full components \(\mathfrak G_v\) contains an object over \(a\).  By
\Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(ii), this is equivalent to
neutrality of at least one component gerbe.  Negating that statement while
retaining nonemptiness of \((aF)(a)\) proves
\eqref{eq:matching-gerbe-criterion}; the cohomological form follows from the
same standard package.
\end{proof}

The criterion is a site-theoretic reformulation of the meet-semilattice
comparison in the companion manuscript
\cite[Theorem~5.20]{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure}; it is not an
independent gerbe-classification theorem.  Its role here is to connect the
strict presheaf predicate to the standard component-gerbe construction on a
pullback site.

We now give the representative-rigidity argument in full.  This result
isolates a restriction on attempts to realize matching without amalgamation
by an excessively strict lift.

\begin{theorem}[Terminal rigidity for representative-rigid lifts]
\label{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}
Let \(F\) be separated on the slice over \(a\), and let \(\mathfrak P\) be a
representative-rigid \(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack lift of \(F\).  Write
\(u:F\to aF\) and \(\iota:\mathfrak P\to\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\).
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\) implies that
      \(u_a:F(a)\to(aF)(a)\) is surjective;
\item if \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\), surjectivity of \(u_a\) implies
      terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\).
\end{enumerate}
Under the same \(H^1\)-vanishing hypothesis, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
\item \(\iota\) is terminally essentially surjective;
\item \(u_a\) is bijective;
\item every strict matching family on every cover of \(a\) has a unique
      amalgamation in \(F(a)\).
\end{enumerate}
Strict pullback stability of the selected representatives is used in
\textup{(i)}.  The converse \textup{(ii)} uses only the standard
\(\mathscr A\)-torsor of isomorphisms and its \(H^1\)-class.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We separate the proof into the forward descent construction, the converse
torsor argument, and the comparison with strict amalgamation.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Forward descent construction.}
Let
\(\xi\in(aF)(a)=\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\).  By the proof of
\Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i), there is a cover
\(\{U_i\to a\}\) and a strict matching family \(t_i\in F(U_i)\)
representing \(\xi\).  Representative-rigidity supplies objects
\[
 y_i=s_{U_i}(t_i)\in\mathfrak P(U_i).
\]
On \(U_{ij}\), strict pullback stability gives literal equalities
\[
 y_i|_{U_{ij}}
 =s_{U_{ij}}(t_i|_{U_{ij}})
 =s_{U_{ij}}(t_j|_{U_{ij}})
 =y_j|_{U_{ij}}.
\]
The identity arrows of these common objects are therefore legitimate
descent arrows.  Their triple composites are identities on the nose, so
stack descent glues the \(y_i\) to an object
\(x_\xi\in\mathfrak L(a)\) with component class \(\xi\).  This is the sole
place where strict pullback stability is used.  With merely
pseudofunctorial representatives, cartesian comparison arrows would enter
and their coherence would have to be controlled separately.

The descent datum just constructed is stronger than the datum provided by
equality of component classes alone.  Equality
\([y_i|_{U_{ij}}]=[y_j|_{U_{ij}}]\) would only guarantee local
isomorphisms after further covers.  Equation
\eqref{eq:strict-representative-pullback} instead gives the same object on
the overlap.  Hence the chosen descent arrow is the identity, not a selected
arrow in a nontrivial Isom torsor.  On \(U_{ijk}\) the descent equation is
\[
 1_{y_k}\circ1_{y_j}=1_{y_i}
\]
after the literal identifications of the three restrictions.  No
\(\mathscr A\)-valued \(2\)-cocycle remains to be killed.  This explains
exactly why strict pullback stability, rather than a componentwise choice of
objects, is the forward hypothesis.

Assume terminal essential surjectivity.  There is
\(y\in\mathfrak P(a)\) and an isomorphism
\(\iota(y)\cong x_\xi\).  Let \(t\in F(a)\) be the component of \(y\).
The component map of \(\iota\) is \(u\), hence
\[
 u_a(t)=[\iota(y)]=[x_\xi]=\xi.
\]
Since \(\xi\) was arbitrary, \(u_a\) is surjective.  This proves (i).

\medskip
\noindent\emph{The converse Isom-torsor argument.}
For (ii), assume \(u_a\) is surjective and take any

 succeeded in 212ms:
\section{Boundary with empirical-model obstructions}
\label{sec:empirical-boundary}

Let \(X\) be a finite set of measurements, let \(O_x\) be a finite nonempty
outcome set for each \(x\in X\), and let \(\mathcal M\) be a finite antichain
of contexts covering \(X\).  For a finite empirical model \(e\), write
\[
 S_e(U)=\operatorname{supp}(e_U)
\]
for its support presheaf on the downward-closed context category.  Adjoin a
terminal object \(X\), declare \(\mathcal M\) to cover it, and set
\[
 \widehat S_e(X)=\varprojlim_{U}S_e(U).
\]
Thus \(\widehat S_e(X)\) is the set of compatible supported local
assignments.  Let \(aS_e\) be the associated sheaf on this terminally
augmented context site.

\subsection*{The terminal augmentation}

On the original downward-closed context category, a global assignment is a
compatible family rather than a section over a named terminal context,
because the full measurement set \(X\) need not belong to the measurement
cover.  The augmentation adjoins \(X\) as a bookkeeping object and declares
the maximal contexts to cover it.  Its value is
\[
 \widehat S_e(X)
 =\left\{(s_C)_{C\in\mathcal M}:
 \begin{array}{l}
 s_C\in S_e(C),\\
 s_C|_{C\cap D}=s_D|_{C\cap D}
 \text{ for all }C,D
 \end{array}\right\}.
\]

Because the event sheaf has unique gluing, a compatible family determines a
unique outcome assignment \(s\in\prod_{x\in X}O_x\) whose restriction to
each \(C\) is \(s_C\).  Conversely, any assignment whose restriction lies
in every specified support gives such a family.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:contextual-global-set}
 \widehat S_e(X)
 \cong
 \left\{s\in\prod_{x\in X}O_x:
 s|_C\in S_e(C)\ \text{for all }C\in\mathcal M\right\}.
\end{equation}

The plus construction for the cover \(\mathcal M\to X\) produces the same
set.  All members of a matching family agree strictly on intersections, and
the event sheaf supplies their unique outcome assignment.  Thus
\[
 (aS_e)(X)\cong\widehat S_e(X).
\]
Strong contextuality therefore gives an empty set of global component
sections in the canonical support construction, not a nonempty set of
components carrying non-neutral gerbes.

\begin{theorem}[Empirical-model boundary]
\label{thm:empirical-model-boundary}
For every finite empirical model \(e\):
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item compatible supported families, globally supported assignments, global
      sections in the Abramsky--Brandenburger sense, and elements of
      \(\widehat S_e(X)\cong(aS_e)(X)\) are naturally the same set.  Hence
      strong contextuality is absence of a matching family at this
      set-valued level, not matching without amalgamation;
\item for every supplied abelian sheaf \(\mathscr A\),
      \[
      \operatorname{Disc}(aS_e)\times\mathsf B\mathscr A
      \]
      is a canonical split \(\mathscr A\)-banded stack lift of \(aS_e\),
      and every one of its component gerbes is neutral;
\item the Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction attached to
      \(s\in S_e(C_0)\) is a relative class
      \[
      \gamma_e(s)\in
      \check H^1(\mathcal M,\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0}),
      \]
      equivalently a torsor class.  It is not the Giraud \(H^2\)-class of a
      component gerbe.  There can be no construction recovering all such
      classes from a component-section map with component sheaf \(aS_e\):
      for a strongly contextual model the latter map has empty domain
      \((aS_e)(X)=\varnothing\), whereas the AMB classes remain indexed by
      local sections;
\item the homological images and the two-component criterion of
      \Cref{thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss} apply to an empirical
      model only after a band and a non-split banded prestack lift have been
      supplied as additional data.  They are not invariants of a bare
      empirical model.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The event presheaf
\(\mathcal E(U)=\prod_{x\in U}O_x\) is a sheaf.  A compatible family
\((s_C)_{C\in\mathcal M}\), with \(s_C\in S_e(C)\), therefore glues
uniquely to an assignment \(s\in\prod_{x\in X}O_x\), and its restrictions
lie in the stated supports.  Conversely, restricting such an assignment
gives the compatible family.  The terminal right Kan extension is the same
limit by definition, and the plus construction on the cover
\(\mathcal M\to X\) gives the same set in \((aS_e)(X)\).  This proves (i)
and agrees with the standard support formulation of contextuality
\cite{AbramskyBrandenburger2011}.

The four descriptions in (i) fit into a commutative square:
\[
\begin{array}{ccc}
\{\text{compatible support families}\}
 &\longrightarrow&
\{\text{outcome assignments locally in the support}\}\\
\big\downarrow&&\big\downarrow\\
\widehat S_e(X)&\longrightarrow&(aS_e)(X).
\end{array}
\]
The upper arrow is unique gluing in \(\mathcal E\), the left arrow is the
definition of the inverse limit, and the lower arrow is the plus-construction
map.  Every arrow is a bijection.  No groupoid or band enters this set-level
statement.

The discrete-groupoid functor sends a sheaf of sets to a stack with that
component sheaf and zero automorphism sheaves.  The classifying stack
\(\mathsf B\mathscr A\) has one component and automorphism sheaf
\(\mathscr A\).  Their product is therefore a split banded lift of
\(aS_e\).  In each component, the identity \(\mathscr A\)-torsor is a
global object, so the component gerbe is neutral and its Giraud class is
zero.  This proves (ii).

Objectwise, the product stack has objects
\[
 (s,T),
 \qquad
 s\in(aS_e)(U),\quad
 T\text{ an }\mathscr A|_U\text{-torsor}.
\]
Two objects can be isomorphic only when their first coordinates agree.
Consequently the component sheaf is \(aS_e\).  The automorphism sheaf of
\((s,T)\) is canonically \(\mathscr A|_U\).  For a global component
section \(s\), the object
\[
 (s,\mathscr A)
\]
with the trivial torsor lies globally in the corresponding full component.
This identifies the neutralizing object directly.

Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa apply the free-abelian presheaf
\(\mathcal F_e=\mathbb Z[S_e]\) and a relative kernel presheaf to obtain
the displayed degree-one Cech class
\cite[Sec.~4]{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012}.  Car\`u identifies this
class with an appropriate torsor trivialized by the measurement cover
\cite[Prop.~6.3]{Caru2017}.  By contrast, the map in
\Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package} is indexed by global sections
of the component sheaf and takes values in derived \(H^2\) of a sheaf band.
If \(e\) is strongly contextual, (i) gives
\((aS_e)(X)=\varnothing\), so every such component-section map has empty
domain.  Local sections \(s\in S_e(C_0)\) can still exist and index AMB
classes.  This degree and indexing mismatch proves (iii).

The mismatch has two independent parts:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 &\text{AMB obstruction}&\text{component-gerbe obstruction}\\ \hline
\text{index}&s\in S_e(C_0)&v\in(aS_e)(X)\\
\text{degree}&\check H^1&H^2\\
\text{object}&\text{relative torsor}&\text{banded gerbe}\\
\text{canonical lift}&\text{relative kernel data}&\text{split stack.}
\end{array}
\]
Changing degree by a formal shift would not repair the indexing difference.
In a strongly contextual model the right-hand index set is empty, while the
left-hand index set can contain many local sections.  When a global support
assignment does exist, the canonical component indexed by it is neutral.
A nonzero degree-two class requires additional lift data in either case.

Finally, the canonical lift in (ii) has zero component classes and zero
homological image subgroups.  A non-split lift, a finite good-cover nerve,
and prescribed maps \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A\) are additional inputs to
\Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}; none is selected by the
support presheaf of a bare empirical model.  This proves (iv).
\end{proof}

\subsection*{Additional data required for the finite-site construction}

To apply the prescribed-realization theorem to an empirical model, one would
have to supply, at minimum:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item a finite intersection site with connected nonempty overlaps and a
      connected nerve;
\item a finite abelian band \(A\);
\item an \(A\)-banded prestack whose component presheaf is related to the
      chosen support data;
\item normalized Cech \(2\)-cocycles, or prescribed maps
      \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A\), for its component labels;
\item the terminal-fibre behavior required by the comparison criterion.
\end{enumerate}
The support presheaf supplies none of (ii)--(iv).  It supplies a canonical
answer only after one chooses the split product stack, and that answer sets
every degree-two component class to zero.  No construction established here
maps bare support presheaves to the non-split data used by the wedge
corollary.

One may postulate such lift data for a particular empirical model and then
apply the article's theorems conditionally.  The resulting homological images
belong to that supplied finite presentation and lift, not to the empirical
model alone.  The distinction is the same as in the opening interface: the
paper begins after the site, band, and prestack have been specified.

The theorem concerns possibilistic support.  Probabilistic
noncontextuality asks for a distribution on the global assignment set with
the given marginals, a different extension problem which is not determined
by \(S_e\).  This distinction is independent of the degree mismatch above.

 succeeded in 235ms:
\section{Finite good-cover sites and prescribed realization}
\label{sec:homological-visibility}

Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a finite open cover of a space
\(X\).  Its ordinary nerve \(N=N(\mathcal U)\) records which finite
intersections are nonempty.  In order to identify Cech cochains with one
copy of a coefficient group for each simplex of \(N\), we impose the
following hypothesis throughout this section:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}
 \begin{minipage}{0.82\textwidth}
 every nonempty finite intersection \(U_J=\bigcap_{j\in J}U_j\) is
 connected, and the chosen trivializations of the locally constant
 coefficient sheaf identify \(\Gamma(U_J,\mathscr A)\) coherently with
 the same group \(A\).
 \end{minipage}
\end{equation}
Without connectedness,
\(\Gamma(U_J,\mathscr A)\cong A^{\pi_0(U_J)}\), whereas the ordinary nerve
retains only the information that \(U_J\) is nonempty.  Thus
\eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis} is essential for the ordinary-nerve
formulation below.  A component nerve or local coefficients would be needed
without it.

For a fixed cover satisfying \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, define
the \emph{finite intersection site} \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\) as follows.
Its objects are a terminal object \(a\), one object \(U_J\) for every
nonempty simplex \(J\) of \(N\), and a strict initial object \(\varnothing\)
representing all empty intersections.  There is an arrow
\(U_J\to U_K\) when \(K\subseteq J\), and
\[
 U_J\times_{U_L}U_K=
 \begin{cases}
 U_{J\cup K},&J\cup K\in N,\\
 \varnothing,&J\cup K\notin N.
 \end{cases}
\]
The pretopology is generated by the vertex cover \(\{U_i\to a\}\), its
pullbacks, identity covers, and the empty cover of \(\varnothing\).  Let
\(A_{\mathcal U}\) be the restriction of the sheaf of locally constant
\(A\)-valued functions on \(X\).  Hence
\[
 A_{\mathcal U}(U_J)=A,
 \qquad A_{\mathcal U}(\varnothing)=0,
\]
under the coherent trivializations in
\eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}.

\subsection*{Pullbacks, generated covers, and the initial object}

The formal initial object is part of the site, even though it contributes
zero to the nonempty Cech nerve.  For an arbitrary finite subset
\(J\subseteq I\), write
\[
 \widetilde U_J=
 \begin{cases}
 U_J,&J\in N,\\
 \varnothing,&J\notin N.
 \end{cases}
\]
If \(U_J\to U_L\) and \(U_K\to U_L\), then
\[
 U_J\times_{U_L}U_K=\widetilde U_{J\cup K}.
\]
Indeed, if \(J\cup K\) is a simplex, every intersection object mapping to
both factors is indexed by a simplex containing \(J\cup K\), and therefore
factors uniquely through \(U_{J\cup K}\).  If it is not a simplex, no
nonempty object maps to both factors and the universal object is
\(\varnothing\).  Pullbacks involving \(\varnothing\) are again
\(\varnothing\).  Thus disjoint cover members still have a fibre product in
the site.

Pulling the vertex cover back along \(U_J\to a\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:formal-pulled-back-cover}
 \{\widetilde U_{J\cup\{i\}}\longrightarrow U_J\}_{i\in I}.
\end{equation}
Terms equal to \(\varnothing\) may be repeated, but they are not silently
deleted from the pullback calculation.  They make no contribution to
matching for \(A_{\mathcal U}\) because the empty cover forces
\(A_{\mathcal U}(\varnothing)=0\).  The generated pretopology is obtained
by iterating \eqref{eq:formal-pulled-back-cover}, identity covers, and
transitivity.

There is a useful simplification away from the terminal object.  If
\(U_J\ne a,\varnothing\), then the pulled-back family contains the identity
of \(U_J\): choose any \(i\in J\), for which
\(\widetilde U_{J\cup\{i\}}=U_J\).  Every generated cover of a nonempty
object in the slice over \(U_J\) consequently refines a cover containing an
identity.  Descent for an abelian sheaf on this slice is therefore controlled
by its value at the terminal slice object.  Evaluation at that object is an
exact functor, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:formal-slice-acyclicity}
 H^q(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/U_J,A_{\mathcal U})=0
 \qquad(q>0).
\end{equation}
This supplies the Leray acyclicity internally for the formal good-cover
site.  If one begins with an actual topological good cover instead, the same
vanishing follows because every nonempty intersection is contractible.

\subsection*{Why connectedness is the exact ordinary-nerve hypothesis}

For a sheaf of locally constant \(A\)-valued functions,
\[
 \Gamma(U_J,A_X)\cong
 \prod_{C\in\pi_0(U_J)}A.
\]
An ordinary \(q\)-simplex \(J=\{i_0,\dots,i_q\}\) of \(N\) records only
that \(U_J\) is nonempty.  It does not record the set
\(\pi_0(U_J)\).  Hence an ordinary simplicial cochain assigns one element of
\(A\) to \(J\), while a Cech cochain assigns one element for every connected
component of \(U_J\).  The complexes coincide precisely under coherent
identifications
\[
 \Gamma(U_J,A_X)\cong A
\]
on every nonempty overlap.  Connectedness supplies the single copy, and the
chosen trivializations ensure that all face restrictions are the identity
of the same coefficient group.  This explains both parts of
\eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}; neither is only a notational
convenience.

Under these identifications, an ordered Cech cochain
\(c\in\check C^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})\) is the family
\[
 c_{i_0\cdots i_q}\in A
 \qquad
 (i_0<\cdots<i_q,\ U_{i_0\cdots i_q}\ne\varnothing).
\]
Its differential is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cech-simplicial-differential}
 (\delta c)_{i_0\cdots i_{q+1}}
 =\sum_{r=0}^{q+1}(-1)^r
 c_{i_0\cdots\widehat{i_r}\cdots i_{q+1}},
\end{equation}
which is exactly the simplicial coboundary of \(N\).  If an overlap is
empty, its section group is zero and there is no corresponding simplex.
This gives the cochain-level, rather than merely cohomological,
identification used by the realization theorem.

\begin{proposition}[Connected-overlap and basis-site comparison]
\label{prop:finite-site-comparison}
Let \(A\) be an abelian group.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item For a finite cover satisfying
      \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, the ordered Cech complex of
      \(A_{\mathcal U}\) is naturally the simplicial cochain complex of the
      ordinary nerve:
      \[
      \check C^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})
      \cong C^q(N,A).
      \]
\item If the nonempty intersections are also
      \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-acyclic, the Leray comparison gives
      \[
      H^q(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U},A_{\mathcal U})
      \cong \check H^q(\mathcal U,A_{\mathcal U})
      \cong H^q(N,A).
      \]
\item Let \(K\) be a finite simplicial complex, put \(X=|K|\), and let
      \(\mathcal U^{(m)}\) be the open-star cover of
      \(\operatorname{Sd}^mK\).  The category \(\mathcal B_K\) of all open
      stars in these covers and all their finite intersections is a basis
      for \(X\), stable under finite intersections after adjoining the empty
      set.  The topology generated on \(\mathcal B_K\) is the induced
      open-cover topology, and restriction gives an equivalence
      \[
      \mathbf {Sh}(X)\simeq\mathbf {Sh}(\mathcal B_K).
      \]
      Under this equivalence the genuine constant sheaf on
      \(\mathcal B_K\) is the restriction of the ordinary constant sheaf on
      \(X\).  Every nonempty basic intersection is contractible, the covers
      \((\mathcal U^{(m)})_{m\ge0}\) are filtered and cofinal, and
      \[
      \varinjlim_m\check H^2(\mathcal U^{(m)},A)
      \cong H^2(X,A).
      \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For (i), an ordered Cech \(q\)-cochain assigns a section of
\(A_{\mathcal U}\) to each nonempty ordered \((q+1)\)-fold intersection.
Connectedness and the coherent trivializations identify that section group
with one copy of \(A\), and the restriction maps with the identity of
\(A\).  Empty intersections have section group zero.  The ordered Cech
coboundary is therefore exactly the alternating simplicial coboundary
\eqref{eq:cech-simplicial-differential}.  The identification commutes with
restriction to a subcover and with every refinement map compatible with the
chosen coefficient trivializations.

For (ii), the Cech-to-derived spectral sequence for \(\mathcal U\) has
\[
 E_1^{p,q}=\prod_{i_0<\cdots<i_p}
 H^q(U_{i_0\cdots i_p},A_{\mathcal U}).
\]
Acyclicity makes every row with \(q>0\) vanish, so the edge map is an
isomorphism.  Clause (i) identifies its remaining row with the nerve
cochain complex; this is the usual Leray theorem
\cite[Chap.~III, Sec.~4]{Bredon1997}.

For the formal intersection site, the same vanishing is visible directly in
\eqref{eq:formal-slice-acyclicity}.  The augmented Cech resolution
\[
 0\longrightarrow A_{\mathcal U}
 \longrightarrow
 \prod_i(j_i)_*A_{\mathcal U}|_{U_i}
 \longrightarrow
 \prod_{i<j}(j_{ij})_*A_{\mathcal U}|_{U_{ij}}
 \longrightarrow\cdots
\]
has acyclic terms because every nonempty intersection slice has exact global
sections.  Applying global sections computes derived cohomology by the same
ordered complex.  This makes clear that the comparison is a property of the
specified site and band, not of an abstract nerve alone.

For (iii), open stars in successive barycentric subdivisions have arbitrarily
small mesh.  Given \(x\in O\) with \(O\subseteq X\) open, a sufficiently
fine subdivision has a vertex whose open star contains \(x\) and lies in
\(O\).  Hence the union of all these stars is a basis.  Adding their finite
intersections makes it stable under intersections, and a family of basis
objects covers precisely when their union covers in \(X\).  Thus its
generated topology is the topology induced from the open-set site.

To make the small-mesh assertion explicit, fix a metric on the compact
polyhedron \(X\).  Barycentric subdivision reduces the mesh of each simplex
by a uniform factor strictly less than one.  Choose
\(\varepsilon>0\) with \(B(x,\varepsilon)\subseteq O\).  At a sufficiently
late subdivision, every simplex meeting \(x\) has diameter less than
\(\varepsilon/2\).  A vertex belonging to a smallest simplex whose closure
contains \(x\) then has open star contained in \(O\).  Applying this
argument to every member of a finite open cover and using compactness shows
that a sufficiently late star cover refines that cover.

The Comparison Lemma for a basis stable under intersections now gives the
displayed equivalence of sheaf categories; explicitly, restriction is fully
faithful because sections are recovered by gluing over basis covers, and its
quasi-inverse sends a basis sheaf to the sheaf whose sections on an open set
are compatible basis-local sections.  This also shows directly that locally
constant \(A\)-valued functions restrict to the genuine constant sheaf used
on \(\mathcal B_K\) \cite[Chap.~III, Sec.~7]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}.

A nonempty intersection of open stars is the open star of the simplex spanned
by the corresponding vertices, hence is contractible.  Barycentric
subdivision refines the preceding cover, and every finite basis cover is
refined by a sufficiently late subdivision cover; the sequence is therefore
filtered and cofinal.  The basic intersections are acyclic for the constant
sheaf, so (ii), the sheaf-category equivalence, and cofinality give
\[
 \varinjlim_m\check H^2(\mathcal U^{(m)},A)
 \cong H^2(\mathcal B_K,A)
 \cong H^2(X,A).
\]
This proves every asserted comparison, including the passage from the
all-subdivision generated site to the usual topological site.

The order of the argument is essential.  Contractibility supplies
connectedness and acyclicity on each selected cover.  The basis comparison
identifies sheaves on the generated site with sheaves on \(X\).  Cofinality
then identifies the colimit over subdivision covers with the full Cech
colimit.  Only after these three checks is the final derived-cohomology
identification justified.
\end{proof}

We turn to the paper-specific packaging of the standard Cech-cocycle gerbe
construction.  The point is not that a class in \(H^2\) can be represented
by a gerbe; that is classical.  The content is simultaneous control of the
component presheaf, terminal essential surjectivity, neutral and non-neutral
labels, and the images of the evaluation homomorphisms.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-site realization of prescribed componentwise evaluation maps]
\label{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}
Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a finite good cover satisfying
\eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}, with connected nerve \(N\), and let
\(A\) be a finite abelian group.  Assume
\[
 H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0.
\]
Let \(\Lambda\) be a finite nonempty set and prescribe homomorphisms
\[
 \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A
 \qquad(\lambda\in\Lambda).
\]
Put \(\Lambda_0=\{\lambda:\phi_\lambda=0\}\).  Then there are a presheaf
\(F\) on \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\) and an
\(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack lift
\(\mathfrak P\), with stackification \(\mathfrak L\), such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item \(F(a)=\Lambda_0\), \((aF)(a)\cong\Lambda\), and
      \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong\Lambda\);
\item the component gerbe \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) has class
      \(\omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)\) satisfying
      \[
      \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda,
      \qquad
      \operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_\lambda})
      =\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda),
      \]
      and \(\mathfrak G_\lambda\) is neutral exactly when
      \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\);
\item \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially surjective, and
      its terminal objects occur exactly in the components indexed by
      \(\Lambda_0\);
\item \(F\) has a strict matching family on \(\mathcal U\), and
      \[
      \operatorname{MWA}(F,a)
      \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
      \Lambda_0=\varnothing.
      \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The universal coefficient sequence and \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) give a natural
isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uct-realization-isomorphism}
 \eta_A:H^2(N,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A).
\end{equation}
For every \(\lambda\), let \(\omega_\lambda\) be the inverse image of
\(\phi_\lambda\).  Choose a normalized simplicial cocycle
\(\alpha_\lambda\in Z^2(N,A)\) representing it, choosing
\(\alpha_\lambda=0\) when \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\).  Extend it alternately
to ordered triples and set it to zero on triples with a repeated index.

The choice \(\alpha_\lambda=0\) for a zero map is legitimate because
\(\eta_A\) is injective: \(\phi_\lambda=0\) implies
\(\omega_\lambda=0\).  Conversely, a nonzero prescribed map has a nonzero
class.  For pairwise distinct indices, alternating extension means
\[
 \alpha_{\lambda,\sigma(i)\sigma(j)\sigma(k)}
 =\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma)\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}.
\]

 =\sum_{r=0}^{q+1}(-1)^r
